6. Mechanistic Safety Assessment
6.1 Study MIT-Es0001-C106 (QTc Study)
Title: A phase 1, randomized, double-blind, multiple-dose, parallel group with a nested crossover
design study to investigate the effects of therapeutic and supratherapeutic concentrations of estetrol (E4)
in combination with drospirenone (DRSP) on the heart rate corrected QT interval (QTc) in healthy
women
Objectives:
• To evaluate the effects of therapeutic and supratherapeutic concentrations of estetrol (E4) in
combination with drospirenone (DRSP) on the heart rate (HR)-corrected QT interval (QTc) on
Day 10 after dosing with 15 mg E4/3 mg DRSP and on Day 20 following 10 days of dosing with
up to a maximum of 75 mg E4/15 mg DRSP
• To assess the pharmacokinetics (PK) of E4/DRSP, following administration of therapeutic and
supratherapeutic multiple oral doses in healthy subjects.
Study Design:
This was a placebo- and active-controlled multiple-dose, parallel group study in 64 healthy adult female
subjects to evaluate the effects of therapeutic and supratherapeutic concentrations of E4 in combination
with DRSP on the heart rate (HR) corrected QT interval (QTc). Subjects in Group 1 (N = 32) received a
therapeutic dose of E4/DRSP (15/3 mg) once daily for 10 days followed by a supratherapeutic dose
(75/15 mg) once daily for another 10 days. Subjects in Group 2 received 400 mg oral moxifloxacin once
on Day 1 or once on Day 21 (N =16 each group). PK determination was performed predose and at 0.5, 1,
1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, and 24 hours postdose on Days 1, 10, 20, and 21. Plasma samples were analyzed for
E4/DRSP on Days 10 and 20 in Group 1 and for moxifloxacin on Day 1 (Group 2A) or Day 21 (Group
2B). The analysis of E4/DRSP in plasma samples was performed using validated LC-MS/MS methods.
Bioanalytical report is MTA243UF-182432-A for E4 and MTA243UF-182432-B for DRSP. Method
validation report is MHUEC302-161697-B (+amendment) for E4, and MHUEC302-161697-E
(+amendment) for DRSP.
Group 1:
• Day 1: One over-encapsulated tablet of 15 mg E4/3 mg DRSP and 1 placebo capsule matching overencapsulated moxifloxacin, administered in the morning
• Day 2 to 10: One over-encapsulated tablet of 15 mg E4/3 mg DRSP, administered in the morning
• Day 11 to 20: 75 mg E4/15 mg DRSP (i.e. five over-encapsulated tablets of 15 mg E4/3 mg DRSP) ,
administered in the morning
• Day 21: One placebo capsule matching over-encapsulated moxifloxacin, administered in the morning
Group 2A:
• Day 1: One placebo over-encapsulated tablet matching over-encapsulated E4/DRSP and 1 overencapsulated tablet of 400 mg moxifloxacin, administered in the morning
• Day 2 to 10: One placebo capsule matching over-encapsulated E4/DRSP, administered in the morning
• Day 11 to 20: Five placebo capsules matching over-encapsulated E4/DRSP, administered in the morning
• Day 21: One placebo capsule matching over-encapsulated moxifloxacin, administered in the morning

30

Reference ID: 4760810

Group 2B:
• Day 1: One placebo capsule matching over-encapsulated E4/DRSP and 1 placebo capsule matching
over-encapsulated moxifloxacin, administered in the morning
· Day 2 to 10: One placebo capsule matching over-encapsulated E4/DRSP, administered in the morning
· Day 11 to20: Five placebo capsules matching over-encapsulated E4/DRSP, administered in the morning
• Day 21: One over-encapsulated tablet of 400 mg moxifloxacin, administered in the morning
Results:
E4 plasma concentrations following multiple dose administrations of E4/DRSP 15/3 mg and 75/15 mg are
presented in Figure 1. E4 was rapidly absorbed following oral dosing with a median Tmax of 1 hour on
both Day 10 after therapeutic treatment and Day 20 after supratherapeutic treatment. A 5-fold increase in
the amount of E4 administered between Day 10 and Day 20 resulted in a 5.4-fold increase in Cmax and a
4.5-fold increase in AUC0-11as1from Day 10 (therapeutic) to Day 20 (supratherapeutic) (Table 1).
DRSP was also rapidly absorbed after oral administration with a median Tmax of 1.55 hours on
Day 10 after therapeutic treatment and 2 hours on Day 20 after supratherapeutic treatment. A 5-fold
increase in the amount of DRSP administered between Day 10 and Day 20 resulted in a 5.4-fold increase
in Cmax and a 5.8-fold increase in AUC0-t1as1 between Day 10 (therapeutic) to Day 20 (supratherapeutic).

administrations of E4/DRSP 15/3 mg and 75/15 mg (Study MIT-Es0001-Cl06)

Safety:
A total of 183 TEAEs were reported by 47 (73.4%) subjects.
• The system organ classes (SOCs) with the greatest number of reported events were
gastrointestinal disorders (31 subjects, 48.4%), nervous system disorders (27 subjects,
42.2%), and reproductive system and breast disorders (10 subjects,15.6%).
• Of the 47 subjects who reported TEAEs, 26 (40.6%) subjects had TEAEs that were
considered by the investigator to be likely related to study drug, and 1 (1.6%) subject had
a TEAE that was considered by the investigator to be definitely related to study drug.
• Two subjects each experienced an SAEs (complicated migraine which required
hospitalization and deep vein thrombosis which was considered an important medical
event); 1 of these subjects also had study drug withdrawn due to the AE (complicated
migraine). An additional subject who received placebo had study drug withdrawn due to
a TEAE of elevated liver enzymes. No deaths were reported.
Reviewer’s Notes: Per the IRT-QT Review by Dr. Girish Bende (DARRTS on August 31, 2020), the
therapeutic (15/3 mg) and supratherapeutic (75/15 mg) E4/DRSP doses did not lead to significant QTc
interval prolongations.
7. Combined Review for In vitro Studies
E4 displays moderate binding to human plasma protein (45.4% and 50.4%) in female human plasma. E4
does not bind to human sex hormone binding globulin (SHBG). E4 equally distributes between plasma
and blood cells in human blood. E4 is extensively metabolized by human hepatocytes in vitro. The two
main human metabolites were identified as E4-3-glucuronide and E4-16-glucuronide, with E4-16glucuronide as the quantitatively most important. CYP450 enzymes do not appear to play a major role in
the in vitro hepatic metabolism of E4. UGT2B7 is the dominant Uridine 5'-diphosphoglucuronosyltransferase (UGT) isoform that catalyzes the biotransformation of E4 into a direct
glucuronide and SULT1E1 is the dominant sulfotransferase (SULT) isoform catalyzing E4 sulfation. E4
does not inhibit the major CYP450 isoforms, does not induce CYP1A2, 2B6 and 3A4, and does not
suggest a clinically relevant inhibition of UGT. Bidirectional transport studies indicate that E4 is a
medium to high permeability compound. E4 is a potential substrate for both efflux ABC transporters P-gp
and BCRP but does not inhibit these transporters. While it cannot be entirely excluded that E4 acts as a
substrate for OAT3, the importance of this transporter in the cellular uptake of E4 is considered to be low.
Distribution
Study ES-T33
Title: In vitro binding of estetrol to plasma proteins by equilibrium dialysis
Method: The in vitro plasma protein binding (from mouse, rat, monkey and human) was measured and
compared using equilibrium dialysis. Specifically, plasma samples from females of each selected
species were spiked at the test item concentrations of 1, 10, 100 and 1000 ng/mL for mouse, rat, and
monkey and 1, 5, 10 and 50 ng/mL for human. Then, each spiked plasma was dialyzed against PBS
solution (pH 7.4) at 37°C in a multi-dialysis system. Membranes with a "cut-off" of 5000 Daltons and a
dialysis time of 6 hours were used for all species/concentrations. The time of equilibrium was determined
to be between 3 and 6 hours.

33

Reference ID: 4760810

Result: The binding of E4 to plasma proteins in samples from women was 47.8% ± 1.4%, 45.5%± 0.4%,
45.4%± 2.6% and 50.4% ± 0.8% at 1, 5, 10 and 50 ng/mL, respectively; and there was no indication of a
concentration-relationship for any of the evaluated species.
Conclusion: The protein binding of E4 ranged between 45.4% and 50.4% in human plasma
Study PR3025
Title: Relative Binding Affinity of Estetrol to Human Sex Hormone-binding Globulin
Method: An established competitive steroid-binding assay was used to determine the relative binding
affinity of estetrol for human SHBG.
Result:
Table I. Relative binding affinities of steroid ligands for human SHBG when compared to
dihydrotestosterone (DHT) or estradiol.

Conclusion: E4 does not binding to SHBG.
Study 0031-104
Title: Estetrol: In vitro plasma/blood cell partitioning in human
Method: Whole blood samples from female human were pooled and spiked with 14C-Estetrol at
nominal concentrations of 0.1 and 1 μM and were incubated at 37°C on a rotary mixer for
0, 10, 30, 60 and 120 minutes. After incubation, a packed cell volume (haematocrit) determination
was made and duplicate aliquots of blood were analyzed for radioactivity. The remainder of each
blood sample was centrifuged. Duplicate aliquots of plasma were analyzed for radioactivity using
liquid scintillation counting, and these data were used to determine the blood:plasma ratio and the
amount of whole blood radioactivity associated with blood cells.
Result: The blood:plasma ratio and mean value for % radioactivity associated with blood cells at 120
minutes for human are summarized below:

34

Reference ID: 4760810

Conclusion: 14C-E4 is approximately equally distributed between blood cells and plasma. The distribution
is not time/concentration dependent.
Metabolism
Study PR3041
Title: Species comparison of the metabolite profile of estetrol using rat and human primary hepatocytes in
vitro
Method: Freshly isolated (one female rat) or cryopreserved (human; one female donor) hepatocytes in
suspension were incubated with estetrol at 1 μM or and 10 μM in a 4-hour time-course study. Unchanged
parent compound and metabolites were detected, characterized and profiled using liquid
chromatography/mass spectrometry (LC/MS) analysis.
Result: The mean percentage of unchanged estetrol remaining in the hepatocyte incubations at the end of
the 4-hour incubation period was 27% and 49% in human, at initial substrate concentrations of 1 μM and
10 μM, respectively. Estetrol was metabolized to at least five metabolites (M1 to M5). The following
metabolic pathways have been proposed to account for the various putative metabolites observed:
methylation, hydroxylation, dehydroxylation, glucuronidation and sulfation. Alternative pathways that
could not be ruled out included dehydrogenation, dihydroxylation and disulfation.
Conclusion: E4 was extensively metabolized into glucuronide and sulfate conjugates by human
cryopreserved hepatocytes.
Study ES-T06
Title: E4: Metabolism in cryopreserved hepatocytes from mouse, rat, rabbit, monkey and human
Method: E4 was incubated with mouse, rat, rabbit and monkey cryopreserved hepatocytes at a
concentration of 10 μM and with human cryopreserved hepatocytes at a concentration of 1 μM. Samples
were removed after 0, 1, 2 and 4 hours and quenched with acetonitrile. All samples incubated with E4
were analyzed via LC-MS and all major metabolites were characterized. Each batch of hepatocytes was
also incubated with [14C]7-ethoxycoumarin as a positive control.
Result: Analysis of the samples from all species identified 13 metabolites of E4. Metabolites
corresponded to conjugation reactions of E4, with glucuronidation and sulphation being predominant.
Only two metabolites were identified following incubation with human cryopreserved hepatocytes and
these metabolites corresponded to direct glucuronidation and direct sulphation of E4. These metabolites
were also formed by the other species. For metabolite M1, the site of glucuronide conjugation
was identified as being at the aromatic hydroxyl group. For the remaining metabolites, identification of
the specific sites of modification was not possible due to low levels of metabolites present in the
incubation.
Conclusion: Glucuronide and sulphate conjugates are the predominant metabolites of E4.
35

Reference ID: 4760810

Study 0031-102
Title: Estetrol: Identification of the major drug metabolizing enzymes (CYP450) involved in human
hepatic metabolism in vitro (CYP450 Reaction Phenotyping)
Method:
• 14C-Estetrol was incubated at a final incubation concentration of 3, 5 and 10 μM with pooled human
liver microsomes at protein concentrations of 0.1, 0.25, 0.5 and 1 mg/mL for 60 minutes and at a
protein concentrations of 0.25 mg/mL for 0, 10, 20, 30, 45 and 60 minutes at 37°C and in the
presence of uridine diphosphoglucuronic acid (UDPGA, final concentration of 2 mM) to determine
optimal incubation conditions.
• 14C-Estetrol was incubated at a final concentration of 5 μM with recombinant cDNA expressed CYPs
1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6 and 3A4 (125 pmol/mL) in the presence and absence of NADPH (1
mM) for 0 and 15 minutes.
Result:
• Following the incubation of 14C-Estetrol with pooled human liver microsomes in the presence of
NADPH, no components other than parent estetrol were detected using flow-through radio-HPLC
analysis
• A small amount of a single component (M1) was found in incubations with recombinant cDNA
expressed CYP3A4. Considering minimal amounts of M1 were detected in all CYPs incubations
including no cofactor controls, M1 may not be metabolically derived.
Conclusion: CYP450 enzymes are not of importance in the in vitro hepatic metabolism of estetrol in
human liver.
Study 0031-106
Title: Estetrol: Identification of the major UGT drug metabolizing enzymes involved in human hepatic
metabolism in vitro (UGT Reaction Phenotyping)
Method:
• 14C-Estetrol was incubated at a final incubation concentration of 3, 5 and 10 μM with pooled human
liver microsomes at protein concentrations of 0.1, 0.25, 0.5 and 1 mg/mL for 60 minutes and at a
protein concentrations of 0.25 mg/mL for 0, 10, 20, 30, 45 and 60 minutes at 37°C and in the
presence of uridine diphosphoglucuronic acid (UDPGA, final concentration of 2 mM) to determine
optimal incubation conditions. Correlation analysis was then performed using five hepatic UGTselective activities (UGT1A1, 1A4, 1A6, 1A9 and 2B7) using liver microsomes prepared from single
human donor livers (n = 16).
• 14C-Estetrol was incubated at a final concentration of 10 μM with recombinant cDNA expressed
human rUGT enzymes (UGTs 1A1, 1A3, 1A4, 1A6, 1A7, 1A8, 1A9, 1A10, 2B4, 2B7, 2B10, 2B15
and 2B17)
Result:
• Metabolism of estetrol and formation of M1 correlated with the activity of UGT2B7 enzyme (R2 =
0.6438). There was also a weaker correlation of estetrol metabolism with UGT1A4 (R2 = 0.4260).
• Only UGT2B7 can catalyze the formation of M1 in vitro. This enzyme is present primarily in liver.
UGT1A1, 1A3, 1A4, 1A6, 1A7, 1A8, 1A9, 1A10, 2B4, 2B10, 2B15 and 2B17 did not metabolize
estetrol to M1
Conclusion:
36

Reference ID: 4760810

UGT2B7 appears to be the dominant form in metabolizing estetrol into a direct glucuronide in human
liver.

Study Es0001-NC-011
Title: Estetrol: Identification of the location of the glucuronide substituent in the glucuronide-estetrol
formed after incubation of Estetrol with UDP-Glucuronosyltransferase 2B7 (UGT2B7)
Method: [3-14C]Estetrol at 10 μM was incubated with microsomes from cells genetically engineered to
express UGT2B7, UDPGA and potassium phosphate buffer, for up to 60 min at 37°C. Incubation samples
were analyzed by HPLC with on-line radiodetection.
Result: Incubations of [3-14C]Estetrol with human UGT2B7 produced a single component in the
presence of cofactor (UDPGA), labelled as M1. M1 had a similar retention time to that of estetrol C-16
glucuronide reference standard.
Conclusion: UGT2B7 is the dominant UDP-glucuronosyltransferase isoform that catalyzes formation of
E-16-glucuronide in vitro.

Study PR3099
Title: Sulfation of Estetrol by Human Cytosolic Sulfotransferases
Method: Human SULT1A1, 1A3, 1B1, 1E1, 1C2, 2A1, or 2B1b, expressed in Escherichia coli and
purified from bacterial cytosol, were incubated with E4 at 37°C, pH 7.4 in the presence of the co-factor
3’-phosphoadenosine 5’-phospho[35S]sulfate.
Result: Sulfotransferases SULT1A3, 1B1 and 1C2 did not conjugate E4. A single conjugate was observed
in incubations with SULT1A1, 1E1, 2A1, and 2B1b, which was tentatively identified as E4-3-sulfate. The
isoform with the lowest Km (Michaelis constant: 0.54 μM) and highest Vmax (41 nmoL/min/mg) for
formation of E4-3- sulfate was SULT1E1. This Km was approximately 13.5-fold higher than the Km of
SULT1E1 for E1 (estrone) or E2. The Km for formation of E4-3-sulfate by SULT1A1, the main
sulfotransferase in human liver, was 15 μM. E1 and E3 showed stronger inhibition of E2 sulfation by
SULT1E1 than E4.
Conclusion: SULT1E1 is the dominant sulfotransferase isoform that catalyzes formation of a direct
sulfate (likely to be E4-3-sulfate) in vitro.

Study 0031-105
Title: Estetrol: Assessment of the potential to inhibit UGT enzymes in vitro

Study 0031- NC017 (Bidirectional transport study in human Caco-2 cells)
Title: Study of the solubility and the in vitro permeability of E4
Method: E4 (2, 10, 50 and 200 μM) were added to either the apical or the basolateral compartment (donor
compartment) of human Caco-2 cell monolayer cultures and incubated for up to 120 minutes. Samples
collection in apical and basal compartments during 2 hours after addition of the test compounds in the
apical or basolateral chamber. At different time-points, sampling, filtering and assay of the samples
39

Reference ID: 4760810

using a LC-MS/MS method after the derivatization reaction.
Result: Controls for cell performance consisted of propranolol permeability, and efflux ratios of [3H]digoxin (P-gp), [3H]-prazosin (breast cancer resistance protein, BCRP) and [14C]-methotrexate (MRP2).
TEER (> 300 Ohm·cm2) and Lucifer Yellow permeability (2.25 x 10-6 cm/sec) showed acceptable
barrier integrity. The overall average apical to basolateral permeability is 1.8 x 10-5 cm/sec, indicating a
high cellular permeability. The overall average efflux ratio was 3.19 (i.e. > 2), suggesting E4 was possibly
a substrate for an efflux transporter.
Conclusion:
E4 is a medium to high permeability compound and may be a substrate for efflux transporters.

Study 0031-103 (Interaction of E4 as a substrate or an inhibitor of human ABC efflux transporters)
Title: Estetrol: Assessment of efflux-mediated transport
Method:
• ABC transporter substrate assessment: Estetrol was incubated (in triplicate) with MDCKII cell lines
transfected with either the human P-gp or BCRP gene, as well as wild-type (WT; non-transfected)
cells at four concentrations, of 0.1, 1, 10 and 30 μM. Samples were taken from the corresponding
receiver compartment (basolateral or apical, respectively) after 30, 45 or 60 minutes of incubation
• ABC transporter inhibitor assessment: an appropriate radio-labeled probe substrate and 0, 0.1, 1, 10
or 30 μM E4 were added to either the apical or the basolateral compartment (donor compartment) of
the cell monolayer cultures. Samples were taken from the corresponding receiver compartment
(basolateral or apical, respectively) after 30, 45 or 60 minutes of incubation. The efflux ratio of probe
substrate was calculated as (Papp (B-A)) / (Papp (A-B)).
Result:
E4 displayed an overall average apical to basolateral permeability of > 4 x 10-6 cm/sec, which classified
E4 as a medium to high permeability compound.
• E4 had no impact on the net efflux ratios of the probe substrates of P-gp and BCRP. E4 was therefore
not an inhibitor of these transporters.
• The net efflux ratio for E4 was > 6 at all time-points where calculable. These net efflux ratios were
reduced to ≤ 1.2 in the presence of the BCRP inhibitor Ko143 (where Papp was fully quantifiable).
These data indicated that E4 acts as a substrate of BCRP. In the substrate identification assay with Pgp, the data set at 60 minutes of incubation was incomplete, as E4 concentrations in the receiver
compartments were above the limit of quantification at 1, 10 and 30 μM. At 30 and 45 minutes of
incubation, net efflux ratios at 1, 10 and 30 (30 minutes only) μM were > 2, but inhibition of these
ratios by verapamil was not consistently observed.
Conclusion:
E4 does not inhibit P-gp or BCRP. E4 is a substrate of BCRP. The substrate status for P-gp is not
conclusive.
Study Es0001-NC-013
Title: Estetrol: P-Glycoprotein (P-gp) Substrate Interactions
Method:
Estetrol was incubated (in triplicate) with P-gp transfected and WT MDCKII cell lines at four
concentrations, of 1.9, 19, 60 and 190 μM. Estetrol was dosed to apical (A) or basolateral (B) well
40

Reference ID: 4760810

compartments in the presence and absence of the reference inhibitor, verapamil (100 μM). Prior to
initiation of the experiment, triplicate samples from the corresponding dosing solutions were sampled as a
measure of the time zero concentration. The transport experiment was initiated by reconstruction of the
receiver and donor compartments into one plate following dosing and t=0 sampling. Subsequent samples
were taken from the donor compartment at 120 minutes and from the receiver compartment at 60, 90 and
120 minutes. The experiment was terminated after 120 minutes by the separation of the receiver and
donor compartments.
Result: Estetrol displayed medium permeability (A – B Papp 3.35 – 4.88 x 10-6 cm/s and 2.43 - 3.40 x
10-6 cm/s) in MDCKII-WT and MDCKII-MDR1 cells respectively, across all concentrations tested, with
good recovery (>70%) observed in all incubation samples. Estetrol is likely to be a substrate of P-gp, as
the net efflux ratio (NER) was ≥2 at all concentrations and timepoints examined. Further, these net
efflux ratios were inhibited by >50% by verapamil, with the exception of that of 19 μM estetrol (60
minutes), 60 μM estetrol (90 minutes) and 190 μM estetrol (120 minutes), where inhibition was 45 - 49%.
E4 displayed an overall average apical to basolateral permeability of > 4 x 10-6 cm/sec, which classified
E4 as a medium to high permeability compound.
Conclusion:
Estetrol is likely a substrate of P-gp, as net efflux ratios were ≥2 and were reduced by 45 – 62%, in the
presence of the known P-gp inhibitor, verapamil.
Study 0031-107
Title: Estetrol: Assessment of uptake transporter interactions
Method:
• SLC transporter inhibition assessment: Estetrol (0, 0.1, 1, 10 and 30 μM) was assessed as a potential
inhibitor of OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT2, MATE1 and MATE2-K in incubations
with HEK293 cells transiently transfected with one SLC transporter and wild-type controls.
Incubations with probe substrate in the absence or presence of an appropriate reference inhibitor
served as a positive control. E4 was designated as a potential inhibitor if the uptake ratio (uptake
ratetransfected / uptake ratewild-type) of probe substrate was ≥ 2 and if this ratio was decreased by
E4.
• SLC transporter substrate assessment: Estetrol (0.1, 1, 10 and 30 μM) was assessed as a potential
substrate of OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT2, MATE1 and MATE2-K in incubations
with HEK293 cells transiently transfected with one SLC transporter and wild-type (WT) controls. E4
was designated as a potential substrate if it displayed an uptake ratio ≥ 2 and if this ratio was reduced
> 50% by reference inhibitor.
Result:
• Transporters OATP1B3, OAT1, OCT2, MATE1 and MATE2-K were not inhibited by E4. For
OATP1B1, the extent of inhibition (46.6%) at the highest evaluated concentration of 30 μM was less
than 50% which was considered insignificant and thus an IC50 was not calculated. E4 inhibited the
transporter OAT3 with an IC50 of 16.5 μM.
• Transporters OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OCT2, MATE1 and MATE2-K did not play a role in the
cellular uptake of E4 (uptake ratio in transfected versus wild-type cells < 2 at all E4 concentrations).
For transporter OAT3, an uptake ratio of 3.09 was noted at a concentration of 1 μM E4, but the
uptake ratio was below 2 at 0.1 μM (1.53), 10 μM (1.95) and 30 μM (1.95). Uptake rate-versus-E4
concentration plots in OAT3-transfected cells indicated that E4 transport was not saturated over the
concentration range investigated and a small deviation from linearity suggested that either the 0.1 μM
or the 1 μM value was an outlier. The average uptake ratio (i.e. 2.14) at four concentrations was close
41

Reference ID: 4760810

to 2. Exclusion of the 1 μM data (uptake ratio furthest from the mean uptake ratio) would lead to the
conclusion that E4 is not a substrate for OAT3.
Conclusion:
E4 does not inhibit SLC transporters OATP1B3, OAT1, OCT2, MATE1 and MATE2-K, but inhibit
OAT3 with IC50 of 16.5 μM.
Reviewer’s Comment: Based on FDA in vitro DDI Guidance, the calculated values for R1 and R1, gut are
less than 1.1 and 11, respectively. Therefore, E4 is unlikely to inhibit OAT3 at the clinically relevant
dose.
8. Population PK and/or PD Analyses
8.1 Population PK (popPK) Analyses
Review Summary
The Applicant submitted a population report entitled “Pharmacokinetic Modeling of Drospirenone and
Estetrol”. In general, the Applicant’s population PK analysis for drospirenone is considered acceptable
with limitations, but the population PK analysis for estetrol is inadequate and inconclusive. The results
from these popPK analyses were not used to support any labeling language or exposure-response analysis.
Objectives: The objectives of the report were to assess the effect of various individual
characteristics/covariates (e.g., body weight, race, smoking, and fed versus fasted status) on the
pharmacokinetics (PK) of estetrol and drospirenone.
Data: The population pharmacokinetic analysis included PK data from healthy volunteers enrolled in
three clinical studies: Es0001-C101, MIT-Es0001-C103, and MIT-Es0001-C302. Dosing regimens and
PK data collections are summarized in Table 1.

Table 1: Studies included for population PK analysis

Source: Appendix Table 1 in Applicant’s PopPK Analysis

MIT-Es0001-C101:
• 1024 of 1052 total samples were used from 26 of 28 total subjects receiving active dose of 15 mg
estetrol in fasted and fed state.
• 1024 of 1052 total samples were used from 26 of 28 total subjects receiving active dose of 3 mg
drospirenone in fasted and fed state.

• 1662 of 1713 total samples were used from 439 of 460 total subjects receiving active dose of 15
mg estetrol once daily in a 24/4-day regimen (i.e. 24 days of active tablets followed by 4 days of
placebo tablets).
• 1663 of 1714 total samples were used from 439 of 460 total subjects receiving active dose of 3
mg drospirenone once daily in a 24/4-day regimen (i.e. 24 days of active tablets followed by 4

consecutive days from Days 15 to 28.
• 1700 of 1740 total samples were used from 39 total subjects receiving active dose of 3, 6, 12, 15
mg drospirenone on Day 1 and after washout of at least 14 days, multiple doses during 14
consecutive days from Days 15 to 28.

Population PK Model for Drospirenone
Base Model: The selected base model for drospirenone was a two-compartment model with first-order
absorption with a lag time for absorption. Relative bioavailability (F1) was fixed to 1 and an
interindividual variability (IIV) on F1 was estimated. IIV was estimated for CL/F, Vc/F, Ka and F1. A
negative covariance was estimated between IIV on CL/F and IIV on Vc/F. An inter-occasion variability
(IOV) was estimated on F1. The residual (unexplained) variability was modeled using a combined
additive and proportional error model.
Figure 1. Schematic for the Final Drospirenone Structural PK Model

Source: Figure 1 on page 22 of Applicant’s population PK report
Final Model: Covariate analysis were conducted with forward addition and backward elimination. The
effects of body weight, race, smoking status, diet, study, dose, and race were tested on volume of
distribution, clearance, Ka, and F1.
45

Reference ID: 4760810

The final drospirenone model was a two-compartment model with first-order absorption with a
lag time for absorption. An IIV was estimated on CL/F, Vc/F and F1 (Figure 1). A negative
covariance was estimated between IIV on CL/F and IIV on Vc/F. An IOV was estimated on F1.
Residual variability was modeled by a combined additive and proportional error model. The
CL/F and volumes were scaled allometrically as depicted in Equation 3, Equation 4, and
Equation 5. Fasted/fed status was found to be a significant covariate on Ka as outlines in section
3.3.2 and in Equation 6.

The parameter estimates from the final model including covariate effects are summarized in Table 3.

Reference ID: 4760810

Note: VPC consists of simulating the trial ~ 1000 times (same patients, same doses, same sampling times), then
comparing the range that the model predicts, assuming the model is “correct” with the observed data. Solid red line
is the median of observed data, dashed red lines are 90% interval for observed data. Solid black line is the median of
simulated predictions, dashed black lines are 90% interval for simulated predictions. Red blocks are 95% confidence
interval for median of simulated predictions. Blue blocks are 95% confidence interval of the simulation predictions.
If the model is “correct”, the observed data should lie near the center of the simulation predicted data.

Source: Figure 8 on page 30 of Applicant’s population PK report
Effect of Covariates on Drospirenone PK Parameters: The secondary pharmacokinetic parameters for
drospirenone are given in Table 4. The secondary pharmacokinetic parameters for drospirenone in Table
4 are representative of a subject with a median body weight of 66.7 kg in a fasted and a fed state. Over the
weight range of 42 kg to 117 kg, the CL/F value is predicted to have of range of 3.59 L/h to 7.74 L/h,
Vc/F will have a range of 53.3 L to 147 L and Vp/F will have a range of 134 L to 372 L. The fasted/fed
status proved to be a significant covariate and was retained in the final drospirenone PopPK model. A
higher Ka (faster absorption) was estimated for fasted status as compared to fed status, which is consistent
with results in the food effect study Es0001-C101.

Table 4: Secondary PK Parameters for 3 mg Drospirenone

Source: Table 4 on page 46 of Applicant’s population PK report
Effect of Covariates on Estetrol Ctrough: The effect of covariates on estetrol cannot be estimated with the
population PK analysis as the model was inadequate and inclusive. A linear mixed ANCOVA analysis
was conducted by the Applicant to evaluate the effect of body weight, race, and smoking status on Ctrough
of estetrol. Ctrough was defined in two sets of data (≥20 hours and ≤28 hours after the last dose and ≥18
hours and ≤30 hours after the last dose). Race and smoking status were not identified as significant
covariates.
Ctrough decreases with body weight at E4 doses of 15 mg, 30 and 75 mg, reaching a statistical
significance for 15 mg in both sets (p=0.0044 and p=0.0020, respectively). An increase of Ctrough
with body weight was observed for the 60-mg E4 dose. This relationship was close to statistical
significance in both sets (p=0.069 and p=0.062, respectively), but should nevertheless be
cautiously interpreted given the limited data (n=10) available at this dose level.
Reviewer’s comments:
The Applicant’s population PK analysis for estetrol is not adequate and inconclusive as to assessment of
the effect of covariates. The Applicant used a two-compartment model to describe the PK profile of
48

Reference ID: 4760810

estetrol, however, the model was not able to explain the evident multiple peak phenomenon likely due to
enterohepatic circulation. The model cannot identify any covariates that can explain the interindividual
variations in key PK parameters. Since the Applicant also acknowledged the flaws and the conclusions
from this analysis were not used for labeling or dosing decisions, the reviewer deems this model to be not
acceptable and will need further development in the future when more data are available.
The Applicant’s population PK analysis for drospirenone is considered acceptable in describing the
observed trend of drospirenone PK profile as shown in the goodness-of-fits plots. However, the predictive
performance of the model was not adequate as indicated in the VPC plots. Model-predicted 5th and 95th
percentile values are lower and higher than those observed, respectively. Therefore, the usefulness of the
model is limited. The model can be used to estimate the PK parameters of drospirenone but can not be
used for simulations.
Independent analysis was conducted by the reviewer to assess the appropriateness of the model and
evaluate the effect of covariates. The reviewer verified the Applicant’s analysis and obtained results that
were consistent with those submitted by the Applicant.
